Search for charginos and neutralinos with B − L B-L R R -parity violating decays in s = 13 \sqrt{s}=13 TeV and 13.6 13.6 TeV p ​ p pp collisions with the ATLAS detector


Abstract
A search is performed for the electroweak pair production of charginos and associated production of a chargino and neutralino, each of which decays through an R R -parity-violating coupling into a lepton and a W W , Z Z , or Higgs boson. This search targets the Higgs boson decay channel of the charginos and neutralinos, using events with three or more b b -tagged jets and one or two electrons or muons. The analyzed data corresponds to an integrated luminosity of 140 fb -1 and 56 fb -1 of proton–proton collision data produced by the Large Hadron Collider at center-of-mass energies of s = 13 \sqrt{s}=13 TeV and s = 13.6 \sqrt{s}=13.6 TeV respectively, collected by the ATLAS experiment between 2015 and 2023. The data are found to be consistent with predictions from the Standard Model. The results are interpreted as limits at 95% confidence level on model-independent cross sections for processes beyond the Standard Model. Limits are also set on the production of charginos and neutralinos for a Minimal Supersymmetric Standard Model with an additional B − L B-L gauge symmetry that is spontaneously broken. Charginos and neutralinos with masses between 150 GeV and 1100 GeV are excluded at 95% confidence level for a scenario in which they decay via Higgs bosons, assuming equal decay branching fractions to each lepton flavor. Additional limits are derived for flavor-specific decay scenarios.

HMBS-2024-09

1
Introduction

Supersymmetry (SUSY) is an extension of the Standard Model (SM)
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
that offers solutions to many open problems in particle physics, including the hierarchy problem
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
. However, the full SUSY model space allows for baryon number (
B
) and lepton number (
L
) violation. While this can lead to rapid proton decay or large flavor-changing neutral currents in processes like
\mathit{{\mu}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mspace{1mu}\to\mspace{1mu}\mathit{{e}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\gamma}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
, these processes can be prevented by requiring conservation of
R
-parity
[
11
]
, a multiplicative quantum number defined as
R=(-1)^{3(B-L)+2s}
, where
s
is the particle’s spin. Enforcing the conservation of
R
-parity (or equivalently, the quantity
B-L
) has additional phenomenological consequences for SUSY models. SM particles have
R=+1
, whereas their SUSY partner particles (“superpartners”) have
R=-1
. Therefore, in
R
-parity conserving SUSY models, superpartners must be pair-produced at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC)
[
12
]
, and the lightest supersymmetric particle (LSP) must be stable.

R
-parity conservation, however, is an ad hoc solution, and may be too restrictive; there are phenomenologically viable
R
-parity violating (RPV) SUSY theories. One such theory, known as the
B-L
Minimal Supersymmetric Standard Model, introduces a
\mathrm{U}(1)_{B-L}
gauge symmetry in addition to the SM
\mathrm{SU}(3)_{C}\times\mathrm{SU}(2)_{L}\times\mathrm{U}(1)_{Y}
gauge group, along with right-handed sterile neutrinos
[
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
. When the superpartners to the right-handed neutrino fields obtain a vacuum expectation value, the
\mathrm{U}(1)_{B-L}
symmetry is spontaneously broken in a way that violates only lepton number, while providing a mechanism to give the left-handed Standard Model neutrinos their small masses. RPV couplings allow direct decay of superpartners to SM particles, including the decay of the LSP, producing signatures that are forbidden in
R
-parity conserving theories. These couplings remain weak, so the production mechanisms of superpartners are not significantly affected.

A wino LSP and next-to-lightest supersymmetric particle (NLSP), superpartners to the electroweak bosons whose primary component comes from the
\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
boson superpartners, provide a unique search scenario for this model due to the possibility of a charged LSP. The charged component, the chargino
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
, and the neutral component, the neutralino
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
, are expected to be nearly mass-degenerate, with less than
200\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}
mass difference
[
17
,
18
]
.
This small mass splitting suppresses
R
-parity conserving decays of the NLSP to the LSP. The chargino undergoes prompt RPV decays into
a Higgs boson and a charged lepton (
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}\rightarrow\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}^{\pm}
),
to a
\mathit{{Z}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
boson and a charged lepton (
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}\rightarrow\mathit{{Z}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}^{\pm}
),
or to a
\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
boson and a neutrino (
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}\rightarrow\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}^{\pm}\mathit{{\nu}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
).
Similarly, the neutralino undergoes prompt decays into
a Higgs boson and a neutrino (
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}\rightarrow\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\nu}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
),
to a
\mathit{{Z}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
boson and a neutrino (
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}\rightarrow\mathit{{Z}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\nu}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
),
or to a
\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
boson and a charged lepton (
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}\rightarrow\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}^{\pm}\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}^{\mp}
).
While the proportion of these decays depends on parameters of the SUSY theory, the branching fractions for decays for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}\rightarrow\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}^{\pm}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}\rightarrow\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\nu}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
are expected to be above 80% for most scenarios
[
18
]
and are therefore targeted in this search. The relative branching fractions of these decays for different lepton flavors depends on the neutrino mass hierarchy
[
18
]
.

This paper presents a search for chargino-chargino pair production (
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
) and chargino-neutralino production (
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
) with RPV
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}\to h\ell^{\pm}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}\to h\nu
decays. The data was collected with the ATLAS detector in proton–proton (
pp
) collisions at the LHC and comprises
140\text{\,}{\mathrm{fb}}^{-1}
at
\sqrt{s}=13

TeV
taken from 2015–2018 (Run 2) and
56\text{\,}{\mathrm{fb}}^{-1}
at
\sqrt{s}=13.6

TeV
taken from 2022–2023 (partial Run 3). This analysis only considers the dominant decay of the Higgs boson into
b\text{-quarks}
(
\mathcal{B}(h\rightarrow b\bar{b})=58\%
). Higgs bosons are reconstructed from pairs of jets, and the selection requires at least three jets to be identified as originating from
b
-hadrons (
b
-tagged jets). Diagrams for the signal model are shown in Figure
1
. In the case of
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
production, final states should contain two leptons with opposite-sign electric charge, while for
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
production, final states should contain one lepton and missing transverse momentum (
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
, with magnitude
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
), due to the undetected neutrino. While
\tau
-leptons are not explicitly targeted by this search, they are included in the signal sample generation and contribute to the sensitivity through their leptonic decays. The search strategy aims at reconstructing the mass of the chargino
m_{C}
from Higgs–lepton resonances and the transverse mass of the neutralino
m_{N,\text{T}}
from a Higgs boson and the
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
.

((a))

((b))

Figure 1:
Representative diagrams of the signal model processes considered in this analysis with
the chargino–chargino process (
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
) and
the chargino–neutralino process (
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
). The RPV effective vertices are marked with the bilinear RPV coupling
\epsilon
.

A previous ATLAS search for electroweakino pair production with RPV decays targeted chargino decays into
\mathit{{Z}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\ell
with subsequent
Z\rightarrow\ell\ell
by searching for a trilepton resonance using the full Run 2 dataset
[
19
]
.
The search described in this paper provides complementary sensitivity by targeting chargino and neutralino decays into Higgs bosons and leptons, making use of different regions of phase space.

A dedicated series of Run 2 searches for RPV
B-L
scenarios has also been carried out considering the superpartner of the top quark to be the LSP
[
20
,
21
]
. These were optimized for the pair production of top squarks with RPV decays into a bottom quark and charged lepton. The final state signature is a pair of resonances formed from a
b
-jet and a lepton, requiring
\geq 1

b
-jets in the signal region.

This analysis benefits from developments in the capability of identifying jets originating from
b
-hadrons, namely the GN2 algorithm
[
22
]
, which allows significantly better background reduction as compared with the previous algorithm. The analysis also benefits from strong background rejection due to the requirement of reconstructing two Higgs boson candidates. The sensitivity in the
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
channel is driven by low background due to tight selections, while the sensitivity in the
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
channel is due to good mass resolution for the chargino resonance from the reconstruction algorithms.

This paper is organized as follows. The ATLAS detector is described in Section
2
.
The data and simulated samples used are described in Section
3
.
Analysis objects are defined in Section
4
.
The definition of analysis regions and the background estimation strategy are described in Section
5
and Section
6
respectively.
Systematic uncertainties are discussed in Section
7
, and analysis results are shown in Section
8
.
Concluding remarks are made in Section
9
.

2
ATLAS detector

The ATLAS experiment
[
23
,
24
]
at the LHC is a multipurpose particle detector
with a forward–backward symmetric cylindrical geometry and a near
4\pi
coverage in
solid angle.
1
1

1

ATLAS uses a right-handed coordinate system with its origin at the nominal interaction point (IP)
in the center of the detector and the
z
-axis along the beam pipe.
The
x
-axis points from the IP to the center of the LHC ring,
and the
y
-axis points upwards.
Polar coordinates
(r,\phi)
are used in the transverse plane,

\phi
being the azimuthal angle around the
z
-axis.
The pseudorapidity is defined in terms of the polar angle
\theta
as
\eta=-\ln\tan(\theta/2)
and is equal to the rapidity

y=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{E+p_{z}}{E-p_{z}}\right)
in the relativistic limit.
Angular distance is measured in units of
\Delta R\equiv\sqrt{(\Delta y)^{2}+(\Delta\phi)^{2}}
.

It consists of an inner tracking detector (ID) surrounded by a thin superconducting solenoid
providing a
2\text{\,}\mathrm{T}
axial magnetic field, electromagnetic and hadronic calorimeters, and a muon spectrometer (MS).
The inner tracking detector covers the pseudorapidity range
|\eta|<2.5
.
It consists of silicon pixel (including the insertable B-layer), silicon microstrip, and transition radiation tracking detectors.
Lead/liquid-argon (LAr) sampling calorimeters provide electromagnetic (EM) energy measurements
with high granularity within the region
|\eta|<3.2
.
A steel/scintillator-tile hadronic calorimeter covers the central pseudorapidity range (
|\eta|<1.7
).
The endcap and forward regions are instrumented with LAr calorimeters
for EM and hadronic energy measurements up to
|\eta|=4.9
.
The muon spectrometer surrounds the calorimeters and is based on
three large superconducting air-core toroidal magnets with eight coils each.
The field integral of the toroids ranges between
2.0
and
6.0\text{\,}\mathrm{T}\text{\,}\mathrm{m}

across most of the detector.
The muon spectrometer includes a system of precision tracking chambers up to
|\eta|=2.7
and fast detectors for triggering up to
|\eta|=2.4
.
The luminosity is measured mainly by the LUCID–2 detector that is located close to the beampipe.
A two-level trigger system was used to select events
[
25
,
26
]
.
The first-level trigger is implemented in hardware and used a subset of the detector information
to accept events at a rate close to
100\text{\,}\mathrm{kHz}
.
This is followed by a software-based trigger that
reduced the accepted rate of complete events to
1.25\text{\,}\mathrm{kHz}
and
3\text{\,}\mathrm{kHz}
on average in Run 2 and Run 3, respectively,
depending on the data-taking conditions.
A software suite
[
27
]
is used in data simulation, in the reconstruction
and analysis of real and simulated data, in detector operations, and in the trigger and data acquisition
systems of the experiment.

3
Data and simulated samples

This analysis is performed using
pp
collision data collected by the ATLAS experiment during Run 2 of the LHC from 2015 to 2018 and Run 3 of the LHC from 2022 to 2023. The LHC collided protons at a center-of-mass energy of
\sqrt{s}=13

TeV
during Run 2 and
\sqrt{s}=13.6

TeV
during Run 3. The data sample corresponds to a total integrated luminosity of 196
fb
-1
, with 140
fb
-1
from Run 2 and 56
fb
-1
from Run 3. The uncertainty in the 2015–2018 (2022–2023) integrated luminosity is 0.83%
[
28
]
(2.0%
[
29
]
), obtained using the LUCID-2 detector
[
30
]
for the primary luminosity measurements, complemented by measurements using the inner detector and calorimeters. Data quality requirements are applied to ensure that only data with stable LHC beams and operational subdetectors are used
[
31
]
. Data were collected using triggers requiring the presence of an electron or a muon
[
32
,
33
,
26
]
. The triggers have varying requirements on the identification working point, isolation, and transverse momentum (
p_{\text{T}}
) thresholds of the leptons.

Monte Carlo (MC) simulations are used to model the signals and SM background processes for this analysis. All samples were produced with the ATLAS simulation framework
[
34
,
27
]
based on
Geant4

[
35
]
. The signal samples simulate
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
and
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
production at leading order (LO) with up to two additional partons in the matrix element. In the benchmark model considered, the masses of the chargino and neutralino are set to be equal. The production cross sections are computed at next-to-leading-order (NLO) plus next-to-leading-logarithm (NLL) precision in a limit of mass-degenerate wino
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
and
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
, with all the other sparticles assumed to be heavy and decoupled
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
. These vary from about 2612 fb (5181 fb) for
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
(
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
) production at a chargino and neutralino mass of
150\text{\,}\text{G\mathrm{eV}}
to about 0.62 fb (1.34 fb) for
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
(
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
) production at
1000\text{\,}\text{G\mathrm{eV}}
for Run 2, with slightly higher cross sections for Run 3. The decay branching ratios are set to
\mathcal{B}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}\rightarrow h\ell^{\pm})=100\%
and
\mathcal{B}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}\rightarrow h\nu)=100\%
, with equal branching fractions to each lepton generation.
Samples were generated with
MadGraph5_aMC@NLO
3.5.3
[
39
]

with the
NNPDF3.0nlo

[
40
]
parton distribution function (PDF). The parton shower, hadronization, and underlying event were modeled using
Pythia
8.310
[
41
]
with the A14 set of tuned parameters (tune)
[
42
]
.
The MC setup for the signal, along with the SM background processes, is shown in Table
1
.

The dominant background in this analysis is from top-quark pair production (
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
). The production of
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events was modeled using the

Powheg Box
v2
[
47
,

,

,
50
]

generator at NLO with the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set
and the
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter
2
2

2

The

h_{\text{damp}}
parameter is a resummation damping factor and one of the
parameters that controls the matching of
Powheg
matrix elements to
the parton shower. It thus effectively regulates the
high-
p_{\text{T}}
radiation against which the
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
system recoils.
set
to 1.5
m_{\text{top}}

[
65
]
. The events were interfaced
to
Pythia
8.230(8.308)
[
51
]
for Run 2 (Run 3) to model the parton shower,
hadronization, and underlying event, with parameters set according
to the A14 tune and using the
NNPDF2.3lo

set of PDFs
[
66
]
.

These
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events are separated by particle flavor into
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
with additional light-flavor jets (
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
),
c\text{-jets}
(
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
), or
b\text{-jets}
(
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
). This classification uses generator-level jets, which are reconstructed from stable particles using the anti-
k_{t}
algorithm
[
67
,
68
]
with a radius parameter of
R=0.4
. The filtering classifies the flavor of generator-level jets by using a
\Delta R
matching with heavy-flavor hadrons (excluding hadrons with the top quark or
W
boson as parent particles). Generator-level jets with
p_{\text{T}}>$15\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
and
|\eta|<2.5
are labeled
b
-flavored if they are matched within
\Delta R=0.4
to a
b
-hadron with
p_{\text{T}}>$5\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
, and events containing at least one such jet are labeled
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events. Jets that are not matched to a
b
-hadron but are matched to a
c
-hadron with
p_{\text{T}}>$5\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
within
\Delta R=0.4
are labeled
c
-flavored, and events containing at least one such jet and no
b
-flavored jets are labeled
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events. All
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events that do not contain a
b
-flavored or
c
-flavored jet are classified as
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
events. The
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events are removed from the inclusive
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
sample and are instead modeled with dedicated
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{b}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
samples. The
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
events are removed from the
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{b}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
samples. These
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{b}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events were produced using the
Powheg Box Res

[
43
]
generator at NLO
and
OpenLoops

[
44
,
45
,
46
]
,
and the
NNPDF3.1nlo

[
69
]
PDF set.
The events were interfaced to
Pythia
8.309(8.312) in Run 2 (Run 3)
using the A14 set of tuned parameters
and using the
NNPDF2.3lo
set of PDFs.
The four-flavor scheme was used with the
b\text{-quark}
mass set to
4.95\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
.

The production of
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events was modeled using the

Powheg Box
v2
[
70
]

generator at NLO with the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set.
The production of
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}Z

MadGraph5_aMC@NLO
2.8.1 (3.5.1) generator at NLO with the

NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF in Run 2 (Run 3).
The production of
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}W
events was modeled using
Sherpa
2.2.14
[
60
]
with up to one additional parton at NLO and two additional partons at LO and the
NNPDF3.0nnlo

[
40
]
PDF set. Events from the
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}Z
, and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}W
processes are collectively labeled
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}X
.

Single-top
s
- and
t
-channel production were modeled using the

Powheg Box
v2
[
50
]

generator at NLO in QCD in the five-flavor scheme with the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set, while
\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
samples were generated using
Powheg Box
v2 at NLO interfaced with
Pythia
8.309 (
Pythia
8.308) for Run 2 (Run 3). The
\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
samples used the diagram removal scheme
[
71
]
.
Diboson production was modeled using the
Sherpa
2.2.14 or 2.2.16 generator depending on the process, including off-shell effects and Higgs boson contributions, where appropriate. Fully leptonic final states and semileptonic final states were generated using matrix elements at NLO accuracy in QCD for up to one additional parton and at LO accuracy for up to three additional parton emissions. Samples for
gg\rightarrow VV
were generated for Run 2 using
Sherpa
2.2.2 with LO-accurate matrix elements for up to one additional parton emission for both the cases of fully leptonic and semileptonic final states. The
NNPDF3.0nnlo
PDF set was used for all diboson samples.
The production of
V
+jets
was modeled using

Sherpa
2.2.14 in Run 3 as well as in Run 2
\mathit{{Z}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mspace{1mu}\to\mspace{1mu}\mathit{{\tau}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\tau}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
, and
Sherpa
2.2.11 for the remainder of Run 2 processes, with up to two additional partons at NLO and up to five additional partons at LO.
The virtual QCD corrections were provided by the

OpenLoops
library.
The
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set was used.

For all samples using
Sherpa
, the matrix elements were merged with the parton shower using the
MEPS@NLO
prescription
[
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
]
. For all samples using
Pythia
for the parton shower model, the decays of bottom and charm hadrons were simulated using
EvtGen

[
76
]
. The effect of multiple interactions in the same and neighboring bunch crossings (pile-up) was modeled by overlaying
[
77
]
the simulated hard-scattering event
with inelastic
pp
events generated from a mix of

Epos
2.0.1.4
[
78
]
and
Pythia
8.308.
The
Epos
events were generated with the
Epos
LHC tune
[
79
]

and the
Pythia
events with the A3 tune
[
80
]

and the
NNPDF2.3lo
set of PDFs.

Pythia
pileup events include either a high-
p_{\text{T}}
jet,
a prompt photon, or a lepton from a
b
-hadron decay,
while
Epos
was filtered to simulate all remaining pileup events in the overlay sample.
The individual simulations were first reweighted to ensure a smooth connection across jet
p_{\text{T}}
then
the combination reweighted to match the distribution of the actual number of interactions per bunch crossing (
\mu
) measured in data.

4
Object reconstruction

Charged-particle tracks are required to have
p_{\text{T}}>$0.5\text{\,}\text{G\mathrm{eV}}$

[
81
,
82
,
83
]
. Primary vertex candidates are reconstructed from at least two charged-particle tracks
[
84
]
. To identify the hard-scattering interaction, the event’s primary vertex is chosen as the vertex with the largest sum of the squared track
p_{\text{T}}
(
\sum p_{\text{T,track}}^{2}
).

Electrons, muons, and jets are defined for this analysis at two levels: “baseline” and “signal-quality”. Baseline objects are used as inputs to the
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
calculation and overlap removal, while signal-quality objects are used for event selections. A summary of the
p_{\text{T}}
and
\eta
requirements on signal-quality objects is shown in Table
2
. Electrons are reconstructed as described in Ref.
[
85
]
and are calibrated based on the procedure and results described in Refs.
[
85
,
86
]
. For Run 3, there are additional corrections on the insitu scale and resolution corrections that account for small differences in the ATLAS reconstruction software, increased uncertainties to reflect the Run 3 pileup conditions, and a change in optimal filtering coefficients for the LAr calorimeter readout. Baseline electrons are required to satisfy the
Loose
likelihood identification criteria with at least one B-layer hit
[
87
]
and have
p_{\text{T}}>$10\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
,
|\eta|<2.47
, and a selection of
|z_{0}\sin(\theta)|<$0.5\text{\,}\mathrm{mm}$
on the longitudinal impact parameter
z_{0}
. Baseline electrons that survive the overlap removal procedure described at the end of this section, satisfy the
Loose
(
Tight
) isolation requirements
[
87
]
in Run 2 (Run 3), satisfy the
Tight
identification criteria, have
p_{\text{T}}>$40\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
, and have a transverse impact parameter significance
|d_{0}|/\sigma(d_{0})<5
are labeled as signal-quality electrons. The large
p_{\text{T}}
threshold is used to suppress jets and photons misidentified as electrons.

Muons are identified using tracks in the ID and MS, and are calibrated to match data
[
88
,
89
]
. Baseline muons are required to satisfy the
Medium
identification requirement
[
90
]
and have
p_{\text{T}}>$10\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
,
|\eta|<2.7
, and a selection of
|z_{0}\sin(\theta)|<$0.5\text{\,}\mathrm{mm}$
. For Run 3, baseline muons are also required to have
|\eta|<2.5
. Other selections and requirements match those used in Run 2. Muons with transverse impact parameter
|d_{0}|\geq$0.2\text{\,}\mathrm{mm}$
or longitudinal impact parameter
|z_{0}|\geq$1\text{\,}\mathrm{mm}$
are removed to reject muons originating from cosmic-ray interactions, and events containing baseline muons flagged as bad due to misalignments between the inner detector and muon spectrometer are vetoed. Baseline muons that survive overlap removal, satisfy the
Loose
isolation working point, satisfy the
HighPt
identification criteria
[
90
]
, have
p_{\text{T}}>$40\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
, and have transverse impact parameter significance
|d_{0}|/\sigma(d_{0})<3
are labeled signal-quality muons. Events with signal-quality muons flagged as bad are again removed, as the criteria are different for the
HighPt
working point. The large
p_{\text{T}}
threshold is used to suppress objects misidentified as muons.

Jets are reconstructed using the anti-
k_{t}
algorithm
[
67
,
68
]
with a radius parameter of
R=0.4
. This uses particle-flow objects
[
91
]
as inputs, which are formed using calorimeter energy clusters
[
92
]
and ID tracks.
Calibrations are applied for the jet energy scale and jet energy resolution, and include components derived both from simulation and
in situ
measurements
[
93
]
. Events containing jets from non-collision backgrounds are vetoed
[
94
]
.
Baseline jets are required to have
p_{\text{T}}>$20\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
and
|\eta|<2.8
. Baseline jets are used in the overlap removal described below. Baseline jets that survive overlap removal are subjected to jet vertex tagger (JVT) requirements to suppress pileup, with remaining jets labeled signal-quality jets. Jets with
p_{\text{T}}<60

GeV
and
|\eta|<2.5
are subjected to the
FixedEffPt
JVT working point
[
95
]
while jets with
p_{\text{T}}<$60\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
and
|\eta|>2.5
use the
Loose
forward jet vertex tagger (fJVT) working point
[
96
]
.

Jets containing
b
-hadrons (
b\text{-jets}
) are identified using the GN2 tagger at a working point corresponding to 77% efficiency as measured in
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events
[
22
]
. The tagger uses a transformer-based model utilizing information about secondary vertices and other jet and track properties to identify
b\text{-jets}
.

Baseline photons are reconstructed as described in Ref.
[
85
]
. They are required to satisfy the
Tight
identification working point
[
87
,
85
]
and have
p_{\text{T}}>$25\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
and
|\eta|<2.37
. The
Tight
identification working point removes photons in the crack region of the detector,
1.37<|\eta|<1.52
. Baseline photons are used in the
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
calculation, but not in the overlap removal procedure described below nor in any analysis selections.

The missing transverse momentum
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
, with magnitude
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
, is defined as the negative vector sum of the
p_{\text{T}}
of all selected and calibrated baseline physics objects in the event, with an extra term added to account for soft energy in the event that is not associated with any of the selected objects
[
97
]
. This soft term is calculated from inner detector tracks matched to the primary vertex to make it more resilient to pileup contamination. Jets that fail to meet the JVT requirement are not considered in the
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
calculation, while jets that fail to meet the fJVT requirement are considered.

A procedure is applied to remove overlaps between baseline electrons, muons, and jets. First, any muon that is identified using the calorimeter and shares an ID track with an electron is rejected. Next, any electron sharing an ID track with a remaining muon is removed. If a jet is found to be within
\Delta R=0.2
of a remaining electron, the jet is removed. If an electron is found to be within
\Delta R=0.4
from a remaining jet, the electron is removed. Next, any jet with an associated muon ID track or a muon within
\Delta R=0.2
of its axis is removed if the jet has less than three tracks. Lastly, any muon is removed if it is found to be within
\Delta R=0.4
from any remaining jet.

Table 2:
Summary of the
p_{\text{T}}
and
\eta
requirements for signal-quality objects. Signal-quality electrons and jets use the same requirements in Run 2 and Run 3.

5
Event selection

The event selection for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}$}}\rightarrow h\ell^{\pm}h\ell^{\mp}\rightarrow b\bar{b}\ell^{\pm}b\bar{b}\ell^{\mp}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}$}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\textstyle\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptstyle\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raise 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}$}}\rightarrow h\ell h\nu\rightarrow b\bar{b}\ell b\bar{b}\nu
requires events to have at least four jets, at least two of which must be identified as
b\text{-jets}
, and have at least one electron or muon. If there are two or more electrons or muons in an event, the two leading leptons are required to have opposite charge. Reconstructed signal-quality leptons, defined in Section
4
, have tighter identification working point requirements than applied at online level to ensure high trigger efficiency for selected events. Events are required to have at least one reconstructed signal-quality lepton that activated one of the triggers and has an offline
p_{\text{T}}
higher than a trigger-dependent offline threshold. The lowest
p_{\text{T}}
thresholds are 27
GeV
(27.3
GeV
) for electrons (muons) in 2015–2018, while for 2022–2023 they are 27
GeV
(25.2
GeV
).

5.1
Analysis variables

Higgs boson candidates are reconstructed using four jets. If there are four or more
b\text{-jets}
in an event, the four with the highest
p_{\text{T}}
are used. If there are fewer than four
b\text{-jets}
, all of the
b\text{-jets}
in the event are used, with the highest
p_{\text{T}}
non-
b
-tagged jets used for the remaining Higgs boson candidate jets. The jets are paired to form two Higgs boson candidates by looking for pairs of jets with a small opening angle
\Delta R_{jj}(h)
, where
\Delta R_{jj}(h)
is the angular distance between the two jets from the decay of the Higgs boson
\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and is calculated for each possible pairing. The jet pairing that minimizes
\max(\Delta R_{jj}(h_{1}),\Delta R_{jj}(h_{2}))
is chosen. Studies on generator-level signal MC simulations show that this algorithm makes the correct assignment around 40% of the time for low chargino masses, rising to 95% of the time for high chargino masses. The highest
p_{\text{T}}
Higgs boson candidate is labeled
h_{1}
and the other is labeled
h_{2}
. The reconstructed masses of the Higgs boson candidates are referred to as
m_{h1}
and
m_{h2}
, respectively.

Higgs boson candidates, electrons, muons, and
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
are used to reconstruct chargino and neutralino candidates. First, the four-momentum of each Higgs boson candidate is rescaled by the factor required to yield an invariant mass of
125\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
to improve the mass resolution of the reconstructed charginos and neutralinos, as studied in signal MC simulation. This correction is only applied for the chargino and neutralino reconstruction. In events with two or more charged leptons, the two Higgs boson candidates and the two leading leptons are used to reconstruct two chargino candidates by pairing each Higgs boson candidate with one lepton. Both possible pairings are tested, and the pairing with the minimum mass asymmetry is selected. The mass asymmetry is defined as

A_{C}=\frac{|m_{C1}-m_{C2}|}{m_{C1}+m_{C2}},

(1)

where the
m_{C1}
is the mass of the chargino candidate with the higher reconstructed mass and
m_{C2}
is the mass of the chargino candidate with the lower reconstructed mass. The alternative jet pairing with the maximum mass asymmetry is used to define the variable
m_{C2}^{\text{rej}}
, which corresponds to the lower of the two reconstructed chargino candidate masses in that “rejected” pairing. In events with only one charged lepton, one Higgs boson candidate is paired with the lepton to form a chargino candidate, while the other is paired with the
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
(representing the neutrino in the signal model) to form a neutralino candidate. The 1-lepton channel is optimized to search for high-mass charginos and neutralinos, which tend to each decay into a back-to-back Higgs boson and lepton. The pairing that maximizes the
\Delta R
between the Higgs boson candidate and the charged lepton is therefore used.
As only the transverse component of the neutrino momentum is available (from
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
), the mass of the neutralino cannot be fully reconstructed. Instead, the neutralino’s transverse mass
m_{N,\text{T}}=m_{\text{T}}(h,\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}})
is used. The transverse mass of objects
i,\,j
is calculated as

m_{\text{T}}(i,j)=\sqrt{m_{i}^{2}+m_{j}^{2}+2(E_{\text{T},i}E_{\text{T},j}-p_{\text{T},i}p_{\text{T},j}\cos(\Delta\phi_{ij}))},

(2)

where
\Delta\phi_{ij}
is the transverse angular separation between the objects and
E_{\text{T},i}=\sqrt{m_{i}^{2}+p_{\text{T},i}^{2}}
. The
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
is assumed to come from a massless neutrino.
For both 1-lepton and 2-lepton regions,
H_{\text{T}}
is defined as the scalar sum of
p_{\text{T}}
of the four jets and 1–2 charged leptons used in the chargino and neutralino reconstruction.

5.2
Region definitions

Two signal regions (SRs) are defined for this analysis, SR2
\ell
and SR1
\ell
. These regions are used to search for new physics in a simultaneous fit, described in more detail in Sections
6
and
8
. Their definitions, as well as those of the control and validation regions described in Section
6
, are shown in Table
3
and Table
4
respectively. Each region has a Run 2 and a Run 3 counterpart, using the same definitions.

SR2
\ell
targets
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
production in events with two leptons (2
\ell
). The reconstructed Higgs boson masses,
m_{h1}
and
m_{h2}
are required to be close to the Higgs boson mass of
125\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
. The
m_{h2}
requirements are shifted to lower masses as muons and neutrinos from
b
-hadron decays can lead to an underestimate of the Higgs boson mass. The
H_{\text{T}}
selection is used to reject lower-energy background processes, while the requirement that the dilepton invariant mass
m_{\ell\ell}
is at least
15\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
higher than the
Z
boson mass is used to reject processes with leptonic
Z
decays including
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}Z
,
\mathit{{Z}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
+jets
, and diboson production. The
A_{C}
requirement rejects background processes by ensuring that the two reconstructed chargino masses are consistent. The
m_{C2}^{\text{rej}}
selection reduces
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
backgrounds. SR2
\ell
is binned in
m_{C1}
, using eight bins with lower bin edges of
[125,175,225,300,400,500,600,700]\penalty\ \text{GeV}
, where the last bin includes all events with
m_{C1}>$700\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
. The bins start at
125\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
as the chargino must be heavier than the Higgs boson. The bins are narrower at low masses to capture features of the distribution and wider at high masses to avoid creating bins with very low event counts. The expected pre-fit distribution of
m_{C1}
in Run 2 SR2
\ell
using this binning is shown in Figure
2(a)
.

SR1
\ell
targets
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
production in events with one lepton (1
\ell
). This region makes use of the higher cross section relative to
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
to provide increased signal yields for high-mass signals. Similarly to SR2
\ell
, the two reconstructed Higgs boson candidates are required to have masses consistent with the Higgs boson mass. The
H_{\text{T}}
selection of
800\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
is tighter than for SR2
\ell
, as SR1
\ell
is optimized for higher mass signals that yield higher
H_{\text{T}}
values. The minimum transverse mass of the
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
and any of the three leading
b\text{-jets}
is labeled
m_{\text{T},\text{min}}^{b\text{-jets}}
, computed with the
b\text{-jet}
mass set to
0\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
. For
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events with a single leptonic
W
-boson decay,
m_{\text{T},\text{min}}^{b\text{-jets}}
has an upper bound of
\sqrt{m_{\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{2}-m_{\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{2}}\approx$150\text{\,}\text{G\mathrm{eV}}$
and is used to further reduce the semileptonic
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
background. Events with low
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
are rejected as signals have high
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
values due to the energetic neutrino from the neutralino decay.
The transverse mass of the lepton and
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
(
m_{\text{T}}^{\ell}
, see Equation
2
) is required to be above the
W
boson mass to reject events with leptonic
W
boson decays from processes such as
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}W
, single-top (including
\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
), and diboson production.
SR1
\ell
uses a single bin due to the low predicted event count. The expected pre-fit distribution of
m_{N,\text{T}}
in Run 2 SR1
\ell
is shown in Figure
2(b)
.

. The lower panels show the signal to background ratios. The hatching shows the MC statistical uncertainty in the predicted background yields. The sum of the signal contributions from

and

, with equal decay branching fractions to each lepton flavor. The generator-level signal masses are shown in the legend. Overflow is included in the last bin.

Four regions, referred to as “discovery regions”, are used to obtain results on new physics, independent of a specific signal hypothesis. The first two, SR2
\ell
and SR1
\ell
, are single-bin regions matching the definitions for the SRs in Tables
3
and
4
. In addition, SR2
\ell
700 is defined as SR2
\ell
with an additional requirement of
m_{C1}>$700\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
and is equivalent to the last bin of SR2
\ell
. Finally, SR1
\ell
600 is defined as SR1
\ell
with an additional requirement on the transverse mass of the neutralino
m_{N,\text{T}}>$600\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
. Each of these four regions is defined for both Run 2 and Run 3 as they cannot be combined in a model-independent way due to the different center-of-mass energies.

6
Background estimation

The background is estimated by using the MC simulation samples described in Section
3
. The main backgrounds are from
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
(
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
, and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
) production, and are normalized to data in control regions (CRs). Separate normalizations are used for the 1-lepton and 2-lepton regions due to the different phase space. The largest contribution in the SRs is given by
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
production. Smaller backgrounds that are present in this search are
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}Z
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}W
, single-top,
\mathit{{Z}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
+jets
,
\mathit{{W}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
+jets
, and diboson production. These backgrounds, referred to as minor backgrounds, collectively constitute less than 10% of the pre-fit expected background in SR2
\ell
and less than a third of the pre-fit expected background in SR1
\ell
.

Two CRs, CR2
\ell
2b and CR2
\ell
3b are used to measure the normalizations of dileptonic
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
backgrounds. Separate normalizations are determined for Run 2 and Run 3 due to differences in the experimental setup and simulations. These CRs are defined in Table
3
. CR2
\ell
3b is constructed by inverting the SR2
\ell
selections on
m_{h1}
,
m_{h2}
, and
A_{C}
to select a region with low signal contribution while removing the requirements on
m_{C2}^{\text{rej}}
to further reduce signal contamination and lower the statistical uncertainty. An additional requirement is placed on
m_{C1}
to prevent contributions from signals with large chargino masses.
This region is used to measure a common normalization factor for
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
production, referred to as
t\bar{t}
+HF (heavy-flavor). MC simulation predicts similar relative contributions from
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
in CR2
\ell
3b and SR2
\ell
, allowing a joint measurement with smaller statistical uncertainty. The
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{HF}
purity in this region is over 80%. The expected signal contributions are found to be less than 8% of the pre-fit expected background for any signal mass hypothesis.

CR2
\ell
2b is used to measure the
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
normalization. This uses events with only two
b\text{-jets}
, as
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{b}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events have four true
b\text{-jets}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
events are more likely than
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
events to have a third reconstructed
b\text{-jet}
due to the higher charm mis-tagging rate compared to light jets. Contributions from signals are limited by inverting the SR2
\ell
selection on
m_{C2}^{\text{rej}}
and removing the requirement on
H_{\text{T}}
. An upper bound is placed on
m_{C1}
to prevent contributions from signals with large chargino masses. CR2
\ell
2b has a
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
purity of approximately 80%. The expected signal contributions are found to be less than 2% of the pre-fit expected background for any signal mass hypothesis.

CR1
\ell
3b is used to measure the normalization of semileptonic
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
backgrounds. Separate normalizations are determined for Run 2 and Run 3. This measures a common normalization factor for all semileptonic
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
backgrounds (
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
, and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
), jointly referred to as
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
1
\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}

. These are measured jointly to lower the statistical uncertainty, as the
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
backgrounds are expected to contribute a total of less than one event in SR1
\ell
and therefore do not require separate measurements. The dominant background component in SR1
\ell
and CR1
\ell
3b is
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
. CR1
\ell
3b is defined in Table
4
and is constructed by inverting the SR1
\ell
requirements on
m_{h1}
,
m_{h2}
, and
m_{\text{T}}^{\ell}
to reduce signal contributions. The
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
1
\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}

purity in this region is over 80%, while the expected signal contributions are found to be less than 1% of the pre-fit expected background for any signal mass hypothesis.

Validation regions (VRs) are constructed to test the extrapolation of the background model from the CRs to the SRs. These regions are defined to fall in the phase space between their corresponding CR and SR, as shown in Tables
3
and
4
. VR2
\ell
2b and VR2
\ell
3b test the extrapolations over
m_{C2}^{\text{rej}}
and
m_{h2}
respectively, while VR1
\ell
3b1 and VR1
\ell
3b2 test the extrapolations over
m_{h2}
and
m_{\text{T}}^{\ell}
respectively. The expected signal contributions are found to be less than 12% of the pre-fit expected background in each VR for any signal mass hypothesis.

The agreement between data and predicted backgrounds for different variables are checked in each CR and VR except VR1
\ell
3b2. This region is only checked in a single bin as it has high signal contamination in some kinematic regimes. Reconstructed chargino and Higgs boson masses are shown for the VRs in Figure
3
, where good modeling of the SM background is seen.

6.1
Background-only fit results

Profile-likelihood fits are performed to measure the agreement between data and predicted background.
There are six free-floating background normalization factors in total. Each Run has two normalization factors for
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{HF}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
flavor production in 2-lepton regions and one normalization factor for
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
production in 1-lepton regions. The MC statistical uncertainties and the systematic uncertainties described in Section
7
are treated as nuisance parameters.
Unless otherwise noted, all fits use these six normalization factors and both 1-lepton and 2-lepton regions in both Run 2 and Run 3. All fits are performed using the
pyhf
framework
[
98
,
99
]
.

A background-only fit is performed in the CRs using a single bin for each region. The measured normalization factors are shown in Table
5
.

and

production with one reconstructed lepton.

includes
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}Z
, and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}W
production. Significances are calculated following the method in Ref.
[
100
]
.

7
Systematic uncertainties

Predicted signal and background MC simulation yields are subject to uncertainties. Three types of uncertainties are applied: experimental uncertainties, theoretical uncertainties, and MC statistical uncertainties.

Experimental uncertainties account for systematic effects in ATLAS calibration procedures and efficiency measurements and are applied to all signal and background MC simulations. Experimental jet uncertainties are applied on the jet energy scale and resolution
[
92
,
91
,
93
]
, JVT
[
95
]
and fJVT
[
96
]
, and flavor tagging efficiencies
[
22
]
. Experimental muon uncertainties include uncertainties on the muon isolation, reconstruction, and trigger efficiency correction factors
[
88
,
90
,
89
,
33
,
26
]
. Additional systematic uncertainties affect the muon momenta due to variations in the momentum scale, track resolution, and charge-dependent corrections. For electrons, experimental uncertainties are applied on the identification, isolation, reconstruction, and trigger efficiency correction factors, as well as on the energy scale and resolution
[
101
,
87
,
32
,
26
]
. Scale and resolution uncertainties are applied on the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
soft term
[
97
,
102
]
. Further experimental uncertainties considered are on the pileup conditions
[
103
]
and luminosity
[
28
,
29
]
. Luminosity uncertainties are not applied to
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{b}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
backgrounds as they rely on data-driven normalization measurements. Uncertainties related to electrons,
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
, and most of the jet energy scale and resolution components are correlated between Run 2 and Run 3, while the remaining uncertainties are not correlated between Runs. The largest experimental uncertainties are related to the jet energy resolution. As measured using the background-only fit extrapolated to the signal regions, these are collectively 1.3% (3.5%) of the post-fit yields in Run 2 (Run 3) SR2
\ell
and 23% (35%) of the post-fit yields in the Run 2 (Run 3) SR1
\ell
.

Theoretical uncertainties are applied to the background and signal MC samples. These uncertainties are evaluated by comparing simulations using nominal values of theory parameters with alternative or reweighted samples using variations on these parameters. Uncertainties in the matrix element, parton shower model, factorization and renormalization scales, modeling of initial and final-state radiation, parton distribution functions, and strong coupling constant are applied to the
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
, and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
backgrounds. The matrix element and parton shower uncertainties are calculated by comparing the nominal samples to samples generated with the
Pythia
8
p_{\text{T,hard}}
parameter set to 1.0 and with the
Herwig
7 parton shower model
[
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
]
respectively. In addition, uncertainties on the
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter are applied to the
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+{\geq}1\mathit{{c}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}+\text{light}
backgrounds by comparing the nominal samples to samples produced with the
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter set to 3.0
m_{\text{top}}
. A 50% normalization uncertainty is applied to each of the remaining background samples (
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}V
, single-
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
,
V
+jets
, and diboson). As the background is dominated by
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and
\mathit{{t}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{t}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{b}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 0.0mu\overline{\mkern 0.0mu{b}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}
and the analysis is statistically limited, the uncertainties on these minor backgrounds have no significant impact on the results. Background theoretical uncertainties are correlated between Run 2 and Run 3, but are not correlated between 1-lepton and 2-lepton regions due to the different phase space. The largest theoretical uncertainties in SR2
\ell
are from the parton shower model. As measured using the background-only fit extrapolated to the signal regions, these are collectively 4.2%–6.0% of the post-fit yields in the 2-lepton SRs, depending on the Run. The largest theoretical uncertainties in SR1
\ell
are from the modeling of final-state radiation and the parton shower modeling. As measured using the background-only fit extrapolated to the signal regions, these are each 3%–20% of the post-fit yields in the 1-lepton SRs, depending on the uncertainty and Run. For signal samples, uncertainties on the factorization and renormalization scales, merging scale, parton shower tuning, parton distribution function, and
\alpha_{s}
are considered. The factorization and renormalization scale uncertainties are the largest of these, ranging from 6.1%–11% for
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\mp}_{1}
signals in SR2
\ell
and 8.4%–12.6% for
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{1}
\textstyle\tilde{\chi}^{0}_{1}
signals in SR1
\ell
, depending on the mass point.

The effects of different sources of uncertainties on the SR2
\ell
and SR1
\ell
post-fit background yields are shown in Figure
5
. Background theory uncertainties are larger than experimental uncertainties in Run 2, while for Run 3 experimental uncertainties are larger than theoretical uncertainties in SR1
\ell
and some bins of SR2
\ell
. MC statistical uncertainties are smaller than both background theory and experimental uncertainties. The high-
m_{C1}
bins in SR2
\ell
have low MC event statistics, making the systematic estimates prone to statistical fluctuations.

\ell
. Exp. stands for the experimental uncertainty while MC stat. stands for MC statistical uncertainty. The total uncertainty includes systematic and MC statistical uncertainties. The total uncertainty is not necessarily equal to the quadrature sum of the individual uncertainties due to correlations.

8
Results

Results are obtained using three types of fits. First, the results of the background-only fit described in Section
6
are extrapolated to the SRs to show the level of agreement between data and predicted background. Second, fits are performed in each discovery region to search for evidence for and set contraints on new physics in a model-independent manner. Third, an exclusion fit is performed in the SRs to set limits on the simplified model described in Section
1
.

8.1
Background-only fit results

Results from the background-only fit performed in the CRs (described in Section
6
) are extrapolated to the SRs. The agreement between data and the post-fit background prediction is shown for SR1
\ell
and each bin of SR2
\ell
in Figure
6
. Note that the discovery region SR2
\ell
700 is defined as the last bin of SR2
\ell
. No significant excess is observed. The largest excess observed is in the
125\text{\,}\text{G\mathrm{eV}}
–
175\text{\,}\text{G\mathrm{eV}}
bin of Run 3 SR2
\ell
, with four observed events and
1.24\pm 0.76
predicted background events. This corresponds to a local significance of
1.5\sigma
. The largest deficit occurs in the
400\text{\,}\text{G\mathrm{eV}}
–
500\text{\,}\text{G\mathrm{eV}}
bin of Run 3 SR2
\ell
, with zero observed events and
2.58\pm 0.94
predicted background events. This corresponds to a local significance of
-2.1\sigma
.

8.2
Model-independent results

Model-independent results are obtained for each discovery region of the analysis. A separate fit is performed for each discovery region. For a given fit, CRs and normalization factors corresponding to a different Run or number of leptons than the discovery region are not used. Significances and upper limits are determined respectively using the
q_{0}
and
\tilde{q}_{\mu}
test statistics
[
108
]
based on the profile likelihood ratio. The upper limits are set at 95% confidence level (CL) using the CL
{}_{\text{s}}
prescription
[
109
]
. Regions with non-zero observed events use 50,000 pseudoexperiments to calculate
p
-values, limits, and the confidence level observed for the background-only hypothesis, CL
{}_{\text{b}}
. Regions with zero observed events use 100,000 pseudoexperiments. It is assumed that no signal events enter the CRs. Results are shown in Table
6
. No significant excess is observed, and limits are set on the yield and cross section of beyond-the-SM physics processes.
